Evaluation of transplant benefits with the U.S. Scientific Registry of Transplant Recipients by semiparametric regression of mean residual life
Ge Zhao, Yanyuan Ma, Huazhen Lin, Yi Li, Ge Zhao, Yanyuan Ma, Huazhen Lin, Yi Li

Abstract
Kidney transplantation is the most effective renal replacement therapy for end stage renal disease patients. With the severe shortage of kidney supplies and for the clinical effectiveness of transplantation, patient’s life expectancy post transplantation is used to prioritize patients for transplantation; however, severe comorbidity conditions and old age are the most dominant factors that negatively impact post-transplantation life expectancy, effectively precluding sick or old patients from receiving transplants. It would be crucial to design objective measures to quantify the transplantation benefit by comparing the mean residual life with and without a transplant, after adjusting for comorbidity and demographic conditions. To address this urgent need, we propose a new class of semiparametric covariate-dependent mean residual life models. Our method estimates covariate effects semiparametrically efficiently and the mean residual life function nonparametrically, enabling us to predict the residual life increment potential for any given patient. Our method potentially leads to a more fair system that prioritizes patients who would have the largest residual life gains. Our analysis of the kidney transplant data from the U.S. Scientific Registry of Transplant Recipients also suggests that a single index of covariates summarize well the impacts of multiple covariates, which may facilitate interpretations of each covariate’s effect. Our subgroup analysis further disclosed inequalities in survival gains across groups defined by race, gender and insurance type (reflecting socioeconomic status).

and

1
Introduction

About 15% of American adults have chronic kidney disease
(
Saran et al. 2016
)
, suffering worsened kidney functions with less fluid filtrated by the glomerular, and losing kidney functions gradually but permanently
over the cause of months or years. According to the glomerular
filtration rate (GFR), chronic kidney disease
is classified into five stages, where
stage four (GFR between 15 and 29 ml/min/1.73
m^{2}
)
and stage five (GFR less than 15 ml/min/1.73
m^{2}
) kidney
diseases are considered to be end stage renal disease (ESRD), one of the most lethal diseases globally
(
Ferri 2017
;
Feng et al. 2019
)
. In the U.S.,
more than 600,000 individuals are living with ESRD, about 100,000 new ESRD cases are diagnosed and 50,000 deaths occur each year
(
Salerno et al. 2021
)
.

The most common treatment for ESRD is renal replacement
therapy, including dialysis and kidney transplant.
As dialysis only provides partial kidney functions, dialysis patients tend to have shorter
survival than those receiving kidney transplants, which often lead to a longer and a better quality of life
(
Evans et al. 1985
;
Wolfe et al. 1999
;
Liem et al. 2007
)
. Due to severe shortages in kidney supplies, however, there are far more ESRD patients who
need kidney
transplants than donors available in the U.S.
(
Tonelli et al. 2011
)
. For example, the U.S. Scientific
Registry
of Transplant Recipients (SRTR)
reports that among 247,123 patients awaiting kidney transplants during 2011-2018, only 139,270 patients actually received one, leaving the remaining 107,853 still waiting
(
Hart et al. 2021
)
.

Currently, decisions on patients’ priority of
receiving kidney transplants are based on
the Estimated Post-Transplant Survival (EPTS) score, which predicts a patient’s life expectancy post transplantation by using a Cox model with
age, diabetes status, prior solid organ transplant and time on
dialysis as predictors
(
Time 2012
)
.
Pre-existing conditions such as
diabetes, prior solid organ transplants and long dialysis vintage are associated with
shorter survival
(
Cosio et al. 1998
;
Meier-Kriesche et al. 2000
;
Kasiske et al. 2001
)
; thus patients with these conditions tend to have a lower priority for transplantation
(
Cosio et al. 1998
;
Molnar et al. 2011
)
. On the other hand, younger age is found to be associated with better outcomes and
younger patients are likely to
have a higher priority for transplantation. Thus, age and severe comorbidity conditions have effectively become the most dominant factors when deciding on who to receive transplants, which may preclude older and sicker patients from benefiting from transplantation
(
Jassal, Schaubel and
Fenton 2005
;
Gore et al. 2009
;
Weng et al. 2010
)
. A more comprehensive system, however, should give a higher priority to those who would
benefit more from transplantation among patients with similar conditions, and in the meantime triage candidates who may gain little or even suffer a loss in life expectancy. We propose to quantify the transplant
benefit by comparing the improvement of the patient’s
expected residual life with and without transplantation.
The expected residual
life characterizes the mean of the remaining survival time
given that a patient has survived up to a certain time
(
Hall and Wellner 1981
)
. Compared to
overall survival, the residual life expectancy provides a real time
assessment of transplant benefits at any given time when a
kidney becomes available
(
Lin, Fei and
Li 2016
)
.
As demographic and clinical conditions may be confounders affecting survival and should be
adjusted for when assessing transplant benefits
(
Cosio et al. 1998
;
Carrero et al. 2018
)
,
we aim at modeling and evaluating a patient’s potential
residual life expectancy with or without transplant,
based on the patient’s covariate profile.

Much work on mean residual life models has been sparked by
Oakes and Dasu 1990
. For example,

Maguluri and
Zhang 1994
proposed a univariate
proportional mean residual life model;

Oakes and Dasu 2003
established the theoretical properties
of the methods in
Oakes and Dasu 1990
;

Chen and Cheng 2005
estimated the coefficients of covariates in a proportional mean
residual life model by a partial-score approach, analogous to the partial likelihood
approach;

Chen et al. 2005
employed the inverse probability weighting
approach for inference;

Müller and Zhang 2005
extended the
mean residual life model to incorporate time-varying covariates;
Chen and Cheng 2006

proposed an extended Buckley-James estimator to estimate a linear residual life model and

Chen 2007
further proposed an additive mean residual life
model.
These works inspired median and quantile residual life
models; see for example,
Jeong, Jung and
Costantino 2008
;
Jung, Jeong and
Bandos 2009
;
Ma and Yin 2010
and

Ma and Wei 2012
.
However, all
these works imposed parametric dependency of residual life on covariates as well as how long the patient has lived up to transplantation (or “alive time” hereafter).
Violations of the model assumptions will lead
to biased estimates and incorrect inferences
(
Chen 2007
;
Chen et al. 2005
)
. Our preliminary analysis of the kidney transplant data from SRTR indicates that the mean residual life depends on alive time and patients’ other covariates, such as treatment history, commorbidty conditions and demographics, through a complicated form which is challenging to model
parametrically.

We propose a new class of nonparametric mean residual life models, with the goal of detecting the effects of patients’ covariates
on the residual life and identifying the patients
who may benefit most from transplantation. Our model does not impose any parametric assumptions on
the mean residual life function and, thus, the hazard function, and
extends the model in
Ma and Zhu 2012
and
Ma and Zhu 2013
to
accommodate censoring in response. Moreover, given multiple covariates, we also propose a flexible dimension reduction method to achieve a parsimonious model for efficiency and interpretability.
To derive the estimators, we employ a semiparametric method, in combination with a martingale
treatment as in
Zhao, Ma and Lu 2022
, to derive a semiparametrically efficient estimator
(
Bickel et al. 1994
)
for
the effects of covariates and an asymptotically normally distributed nonparametric
estimator of the mean residual life function.
We apply the proposed method to analyze the SRTR kidney transplant data and quantify
transplantation gains by using the residual life expectancy.
Our
analysis suggested that a single index of
covariates summarize well the impacts of multiple covariates, which may facilitate interpretations of each covariate’s effect. Our subgroup analysis further disclosed inequalities in survival gains across groups defined by
race, gender and insurance type (reflecting socioeconomic status). The results may inform the priority rules for kidney transplantation.

This paper is organized as follows. Section

2
proposes the mean residual life model, and Section
3
derives the estimators for
the proposed model and discusses their properties.
We assess the finite
sample properties
of the methods by simulation studies
in Section
4
and apply it to analyze the kidney
transplant data in
Section
5
. We conclude the paper with some
discussions in Section
6
. We defer the regularity conditions and technical properties to the Supplementary Materials.

2
Semiparametric regression of mean residual life

Denote by
T
the potential time lag from being waitlisted for transplantation (i.e., became eligible) to death in the absence of censoring and by
{\bf X}\in{\cal R}^{p}
the baseline covariates, such as age, diabetes status, and prior solid organ transplant, measured at the waitlisting time. Denote by
W
the time lag from waitlisting to hypothetical transplant time that would have occurred in the absence of censoring. Our focus is to model the difference of the mean residual life with and without transplant at any time point
t
, given
{\bf X}
and
W
.

Let the indicator function
I(W\leq t)

describe the time-dependent transplant status, with
I(W\leq t)=0
and
1

corresponding to “Non-transplant” and “Transplant” at time
t
, respectively.
Following the missing data literature, we use
WI(W\leq t)
to indicate the value of

W

only
when the transplant occurs before
t
.
Given the history of transplantation status up to time t, i.e.,
\{I(W\leq t),WI(W\leq t)\}
, we specify that the conditional hazard at
t
depends only on the transplantation information at
t
, that is,

\displaystyle\lim_{h\rightarrow 0}h^{-1}P\{t\leq T\leq t+h\mid T\geq t,{\bf X},I(W\leq t),WI(W\leq t)\}

(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\{t,{\bf X},I(W\leq t),WI(W\leq t)\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{T}(t-W,{\bf X},W)I(W\leq t)+\lambda_{N}(t,{\bf X})\{1-I(W\leq t)\},

where the subscripts “
T
” and “
N
” respectively stand for “Transplant” and “Non-transplant.” Within the non-transplant group by time
t
, i.e.
t<W
, the hazard function depends on the time and covariates only; at and after transplantation, i.e.
t\geq W
, the hazard function is to be reset and is a function of
t-W
(the time lag since transplantation) because of immediate surgical risks
(
Humar and Matas 2005
;
Hernandez
et al. 2006
)
and long term benefits of receiving functional organs
(
Lin, Fei and
Li 2016
)
. Additionally,
W
is considered as an influential factor in
\lambda_{T}
because, for example, there is a clear survival advantage in favor of preemptive kidney transplantation
(
Liem and Weimar 2009
)
.

A naive mean residual life
(
Maguluri and
Zhang 1994
)
could have been computed as

\displaystyle E(T-t\mid T\geq t,{\bf X},I(W\leq t),WI(W\leq t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\Lambda_{T}(t-W,{\bf X},W)}\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-\Lambda_{T}(s-W,{\bf X},W)}dsI(W\leq t)

\displaystyle+\left\{e^{\Lambda_{N}(t,{\bf X})}\int_{t}^{W}e^{-\Lambda_{N}(s,{\bf X})}ds+\int_{W}^{\infty}e^{-\Lambda_{T}(s-W,{\bf X})}ds\right\}I(W>t),

where
\Lambda_{N}(t,{\bf X})=\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{N}(s,{\bf X})ds
and
\Lambda_{T}(t,{\bf X},W)=\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{T}(s,{\bf X},W)ds
are the two cumulative hazard functions.
However, the conditioning part of this expectation looks beyond
t
for a prospective
W>t
, which is problematic as a patient would be guaranteed to survive at least up to
W
when
W>t
, coinciding with the notion that one cannot directly use
time dependent treatment or, more broadly, “internal” time dependent covariates to predict survival
(
Kalbfleisch and
Prentice 2011
)
.
Instead, at each time
t
, we compute the mean residual life by strictly conditioning on the information available by then, that is,

\displaystyle E(T-t\mid T\geq t,{\bf X},I(W\leq t),WI(W\leq t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\Lambda_{T}(t-W,{\bf X},W)}\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-\Lambda_{T}(s-W,{\bf X},W)}dsI(W\leq t)+e^{\Lambda_{N}(t,{\bf X})}\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-\Lambda_{N}(s,{\bf X})}dsI(W>t),

and will draw inference based on this valid model.

For ease of notation, we rewrite as (
)
as
m\{t,{\bf X},I(W\leq t),WI(W\leq t)\}=m_{T}(t-W,{\bf X},W)I(W\leq t)+m_{N}(t,{\bf X})\{1-I(W\leq t)\}
,
where

\displaystyle m_{T}(t-W,{\bf X},W)=e^{\Lambda_{T}(t-W,{\bf X},W)}\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-\Lambda_{T}(s-W,{\bf X},W)}ds

and

\displaystyle m_{N}(t,{\bf X})=e^{\Lambda_{N}(t,{\bf X})}\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-\Lambda_{N}(s,{\bf X})}ds,

which may facilitate evaluation of the benefits of transplant at any given time.
Particularly,

m_{T}(t-W,{\bf X},W)-m_{N}(t,{\bf X})
quantifies the gain (or loss) of life expectancy of patients at
t
with a transplant given at
W<t
compared with those
who would never receive a transplant; candidates with close to zero or a negative value of
m_{T}(t-W,{\bf X},W)-m_{N}(t,{\bf X})
would benefit little from organ transplantation
and would have lower priorities in the waiting list
(
Chadban et al. 2020
)
.

To ensure estimability, we make a complete follow-up assumption

(
Tsiatis 1990
;
Chen et al. 2005
;
Chen and Cheng 2005
;
Sun and Zhang 2009
)
, that is,
the failure time
T
is supported on a finite range

(0,\tau)
with
\tau<\infty
, where in practice
\tau
is the maximum follow-up time;
we relax this assumption in Supplement
4
. We further assume the covariates
{\bf X}
affect
T
via index
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, where
\boldsymbol{\beta}\in{\cal R}^{p\times d}
is the coefficient matrix with
d\leq p
. Then (
1
) and (
) can be re-expressed as

\displaystyle\lambda\{t,{\bf X},I(W\leq t),WI(W\leq t)\}

(3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{T}(t-W,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)I(W\leq t)+\lambda_{N}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\{1-I(W\leq t)\},

\displaystyle m\{t,{\bf X},I(W\leq t),WI(W\leq t)\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{T}(t-W,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)I(W\leq t)+m_{N}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\{1-I(W\leq t)\},

(4)

where
\lambda_{T},\lambda_{N},m_{T}
and
m_{N}
are unspecified positive functions, which
need to be estimated.
The model stipulates that the conditional mean of
T-t
depends on
{\bf X}
via its
d
indices, formed by projecting
{\bf X}
to the columns of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, and the waiting time
W
.
When
d=1
, the model reduces to a single index model in terms of
{\bf X}
; when
1<d<p
, it corresponds to a dimension reduction structure; when
d=p
, the model is completely
nonparametric. Our analysis first focuses on a fixed
d
,
followed by selecting
d
in a data driven fashion as
discussed in Section
5
.
Model (
4
) is general: it includes the proportional mean
residual life model, i.e.,
m\{t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq t),WI(W\leq t)\}=m_{0}(t)\exp(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})

(
Oakes and Dasu 1990
)

as a special case by specifying

m_{N}\{t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}\}=m_{0}(t)e^{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}}
,
m_{T}\{t-W,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W\}=m_{0}(t)e^{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}+\alpha W}
with
\alpha=0
,
and
d=1
;
it reduces to the additive model
m_{0}(t)+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}

(
Chen 2007
)
by specifying

m_{N}\{t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}\}=m_{0}(t)+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}
,
m_{T}\{t-W,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W\}=m_{0}(t)+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}+\alpha W
with
\alpha=0
, and setting
d=1
.
By allowing
d
to be larger than 1, model
(
4
) extends these classical models by allowing more flexible forms such as

m\{t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq t),WI(W\leq t)\}=m_{0}(t)\{\sum_{k=1}^{d}\exp(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\cdot,k}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}

and

m\{t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq t),WI(W\leq t)\}=m_{0}(t)+\sum_{k=1}^{d}\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\cdot,k}^{\rm T}{\bf X}
,
where
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\cdot,k}
is the
k
th column of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
. These special cases implicitly
assume that transplant or the timing of transplantation does not impact survival.

We further assume that
T
is subject to random
right censoring so that
C\;\,\rule[0.0pt]{0.29999pt}{6.69998pt}\hskip-2.5pt\rule[0.0pt]{6.49994pt}{0.29999pt}\hskip-2.5pt\rule[0.0pt]{0.29999pt}{6.69998pt}\;\,T\mid W,{\bf X}
, where
C
is the censoring time and we observe
Z=\min(T,C)
and
\Delta=I(T\leq C)
. Let the observed
\{{\bf X}_{i},Z_{i},\Delta_{i},W_{i}\}
,
i=1,\dots,n
be independently and identically distributed realizations of
\{{\bf X},Z,\Delta,W\}
. To make (
4
) identifiable and estimable, we fix
the
upper
d\times d
block of

\boldsymbol{\beta}

to be
{\bf I}_{d}
, and
estimate the lower
(p-d)\times d
block of

\boldsymbol{\beta}
. Corresponding to the upper and lower parts of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, we write
{\bf X}=({\bf X}_{u}^{\rm T},{\bf X}_{l}^{\rm T})^{\rm T}
, where
{\bf X}_{u}\in{\cal R}^{d}
and
{\bf X}_{l}\in{\cal R}^{p-d}
.

3
A semiparametrically efficient estimator

Denote the conditional survival function, cumulative hazard
function, hazard function and probability density function
(pdf) of the censoring time
C
by

S_{c}(z,{\bf X})=\hbox{pr}(C\geq z\mid{\bf X})
,

\Lambda_{c}(z,{\bf X})=-\hbox{log}S_{c}(z,{\bf X})
,

\lambda_{c}(z,{\bf X})=\partial\Lambda_{c}(z,{\bf X})/\partial z
and

f_{c}(z,{\bf X})=-\partial S_{c}(z,{\bf X})/\partial z
with

z<\tau
, where

0<\tau<\infty
is the upper bound of the follow-up time.
Let

p({\bf X})\equiv\hbox{pr}(C\leq\tau\mid{\bf X})
,

S_{c}(\tau,{\bf X})=f_{c}(\tau,{\bf X})=p({\bf X})
, and
\lambda_{c}(\tau,{\bf X})=1
.
Here,

\lambda_{c}(z,{\bf X})
and
f_{c}(z,{\bf X})
are absolutely
continuous on
(0,\tau)
, but with
a discontinuity point at
\tau
.

To estimate
m_{T}(t-W,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)-m_{N}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})
, which quantifies the gain (or loss) of mean residual life after
t
with transplant given at
W<t
, we need to estimate

\boldsymbol{\beta}
and the functionals of
m_{T}
and
m_{N}
, for which we consider a likelihood-based approach.

Under independent censoring, the pdf of
\{{\bf X},Z,\Delta,WI(W\leq Z)\}
is

\displaystyle f_{{\bf X},Z,\Delta,W}\{{\bf x},z,\delta,w\}

(5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\lambda_{T}(z-w,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x},w)\}^{\delta}e^{-\int_{0}^{w}\lambda_{N}(s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})ds-\int_{w}^{z}\lambda_{T}(s-w,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x},w)ds}\lambda_{c}(z,{\bf x})^{1-\delta}e^{-\int_{0}^{z}\lambda_{c}(s,{\bf x})ds}

\displaystyle\quad\times f_{{\bf X},W}({\bf x},w)I(w\leq z)

\displaystyle+\{\lambda_{N}(z,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})\}^{\delta}e^{-\int_{0}^{z}\lambda_{N}(s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})ds}\lambda_{c}(z,{\bf x})^{1-\delta}e^{-\int_{0}^{z}\lambda_{c}(s,{\bf x})ds}f_{{\bf X}}({\bf x})I(w>z).

We do not model
W
because the surgery schedule mainly depends on the
organ availability other than
{\bf X}
.
We view the pdf in (
5
) as a semiparametric model
where
all unknown components, except for
\boldsymbol{\beta}
, are infinite dimensional
nuisance parameters. The parameters
\boldsymbol{\beta}
are
parameters of interest with a finite
dimension. We will estimate

\boldsymbol{\beta}
by using
a geometric approach, which
avoids decomposing

\lambda(\cdot)
to be
\lambda_{*}(z)e^{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}}
as in a proportional hazards model.
This entails more flexibility for the model.

Let
Y(t)=I(Z\geq t)
and
N(t)=I(Z\leq t)\Delta
be the at-risk and counting process, respectively. Define the filtration
{\cal F}_{t}
to be
\sigma\{N(u),Y(u),{\bf X},I(W\leq u),WI(W\leq u),0\leq u<t\}
, and let
M(t)=N(t)-\int_{0}^{t}Y(s)\lambda\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}ds
be
the martingale with respect to
{\cal F}_{t}
. In
{\cal F}_{t}
, only
W
that is less than
t
can be observed in order to maintain a valid filtration; otherwise, if
W>t
were to be included, it would introduce unpredictable future information and violate the definition of
{\cal F}_{t}
as a filtration. This principle also clarifies the design choice in model (
1
), where values of
W>t
are treated as missing and not incorporated into the analysis.

Define the score function

{\bf S}_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\{\Delta,Z,{\bf X},I(W\leq Z),WI(W\leq Z)\}=\\
\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\frac{{\bf m}_{12}\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}}{m_{1}\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}+1}\\
-\frac{{\bf m}_{2}\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}}{m\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}}\right]\otimes{\bf X}_{l}dM\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\},

where
m_{1}(s,{\bf v},\cdot,\cdot)\equiv\partial m(s,{\bf v},\cdot,\cdot)/\partial s=\partial m_{T}(s-W,{\bf v},W)/\partial sI(W\leq s)+\partial m_{N}(s,{\bf v})/\partial s\{1-I(W\leq s)\}
,
{\bf m}_{2}(s,{\bf v},\cdot,\cdot)\equiv\partial m(s,{\bf v},\cdot,\cdot)/\partial{\bf v}=\partial m_{T}(s-W,{\bf v},W)/\partial{\bf v}\allowbreak I(W\leq s)+\partial m_{N}(s,{\bf v})/\partial{\bf v}\{1-I(W\leq s)\}
,
{\bf m}_{12}(s,{\bf v},\cdot,\cdot)\equiv\partial{\bf m}_{2}\{s,{\bf v},\cdot,\cdot\}/\partial s=\partial^{2}m_{T}(s-W,{\bf v},W)\allowbreak/\partial s\partial{\bf v}I(W\leq s)+\partial^{2}m_{N}(s,{\bf v})/\partial s\partial{\bf v}\{1-I(W\leq s)\}
almost surely, and
{\bf X}_{l}

is the lower
p-d
components in
{\bf X}
.

As derived in
Supplementary
1.1
, an efficient score is

\displaystyle{\bf S}_{\rm eff}\{\Delta,Z,{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq Z),WI(W\leq Z)\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\{\frac{{\bf m}_{12}\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}}{m_{1}\{s,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}+1}-\frac{{\bf m}_{2}\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}}{m(s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s))}\right\}

\displaystyle\otimes\left[{\bf X}_{l}-\frac{E\left\{{\bf X}_{l}S_{c}(s,{\bf X})\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\right\}}{E\left\{S_{c}(s,{\bf X})\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\right\}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad dM\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\},

based on which, we construct a semiparametrically efficient estimator of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
.

First, a consistent estimating equation can be obtained
from

E[{\bf S}_{\rm eff}\{\Delta,Z,{\bf X},I(W\leq Z),WI(W\leq Z)\}\mid{\bf X}]={\bf 0}
as
the integrand in the above integral is predictable and
M\{s,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}
is a martingale. Hence, to preserve the mean zero
property and to simplify the computation, we can replace

\frac{{\bf m}_{12}\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}}{m_{1}\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}+1}-\frac{{\bf m}_{2}\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}}{m\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}}

by an arbitrary function of
s
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}^{\rm T}{\bf X}
, say
{\bf g}\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}
, and
still obtain

\displaystyle E\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}{\bf g}\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\otimes\left[{\bf X}_{l}-\frac{E\left\{{\bf X}_{l}S_{c}(s,{\bf X})\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}\right\}}{E\left\{S_{c}(s,{\bf X})\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}\right\}}\right]dM\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}\right)={\bf 0}.

This provides a richer class of estimators than the estimator based on

{\bf S}_{\rm eff}
alone.

Second, we can obtain

\displaystyle\frac{E\left\{{\bf X}_{l}Y(t)\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq t),WI(W\leq t)\right\}}{E\left\{Y(t)\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq t),WI(W\leq t)\right\}}=\frac{E\left\{{\bf X}_{l}S_{c}(t,{\bf X})\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}\right\}}{E\left\{S_{c}(t,{\bf X})\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}\right\}},

(6)

where we define (
6
)
to be

E\left\{{\bf X}_{l}p({\bf X})\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}\right\}/E\left\{p({\bf X})\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}\right\}
when
t>\tau
.
We then verify that

\displaystyle E\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}{\bf g}\{s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq s),WI(W\leq s)\}\otimes\left[{\bf X}_{l}-\frac{E\left\{{\bf X}_{l}S_{c}(s,{\bf X})\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}\right\}}{E\left\{S_{c}(s,{\bf X})\mid{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}\right\}}\right]dN(s)\right)={\bf 0}.

The proof of (
6
) and
(
3
) is given
in Supplement
1.1.2
.
This implies that we can construct estimating equations for different transplant status of the form

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{i}{\bf g}\{Z_{i},{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i}),W_{i}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\}

\displaystyle\otimes\left[{\bf X}_{li}-\frac{\widehat{E}\left\{{\bf X}_{li}Y_{i}(Z_{i})\mid\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i}),W_{i}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\right\}}{\widehat{E}\left\{Y_{i}(Z_{i})\mid\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i}),W_{i}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\right\}}\right]={\bf 0}

for any
{\bf g}(\cdot,\cdot)
with

\displaystyle\widehat{E}\left\{Y_{i}(Z_{i})\mid\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i}),W_{i}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\right\}

(9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}K_{h}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},W_{j}-W_{i})I(Z_{j}\geq Z_{i})I(W_{j}\leq Z_{j})}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}K_{h}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},W_{j}-W_{i})I(W_{j}\leq Z_{j})}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})

\displaystyle+\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}K_{h}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i})I(Z_{j}\geq Z_{i})\{1-I(W_{j}\leq Z_{j})\}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}K_{h}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i})\{1-I(W_{j}\leq Z_{j})\}}\{1-I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\},

\displaystyle\widehat{E}\left\{{\bf X}_{li}Y_{i}(Z_{i})\mid\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i}),W_{i}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}K_{h}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},W_{j}-W_{i}){\bf X}_{lj}I(Z_{j}\geq Z_{i})I(W_{j}\leq Z_{j})}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}K_{h}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},W_{j}-W_{i})I(W_{j}\leq Z_{j})}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})

\displaystyle+\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}K_{h}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}){\bf X}_{lj}I(Z_{j}\geq Z_{i})\{1-I(W_{j}\leq Z_{j})\}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}K_{h}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i})\{1-I(W_{j}\leq Z_{j})\}}\{1-I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\},

(10)

with
K_{h}(u)=K(u/h)/h
for univariate
u
and
K_{h}(u_{1},u_{2},...,u_{q})=\prod_{i=1}^{q}K(u_{i}/h_{i})/h_{i}
for multivariate
(u_{1},u_{2},...,u_{q})
with a bandwidth vector

h=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{q})
, where
K(\cdot)
is the standard kernel function
so that
K(u)\geq 0
and
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}K(u)du=1

(
Wand 1994
)
.
Here,
\widehat{E}\{Y_{i}(Z_{i})\mid\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i}),W_{i}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\}\equiv\widehat{E}\{Y_{i}(t)\mid\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i}),W_{i}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\}|_{t=Z_{i}}

and similarly for other terms.

Third,
we obtain the
efficient estimator of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
by solving

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta_{i}\left[\frac{\widehat{\bf m}_{12}\{Z_{i},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i}),W_{i}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\}}{\widehat{m}_{1}\{Z_{i},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i}),W_{i}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\}+1}\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-\frac{\widehat{\bf m}_{2}\{Z_{i},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i}),W_{i}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\}}{\widehat{m}\{Z_{i},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i}),W_{i}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\}}\right]

\displaystyle\otimes\left[{\bf X}_{li}-\frac{\widehat{E}\left\{{\bf X}_{li}Y_{i}(Z_{i})\mid\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i}),W_{i}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\right\}}{\widehat{E}\left\{Y_{i}(Z_{i})\mid\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i},I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i}),W_{i}I(W_{i}\leq Z_{i})\right\}}\right]={\bf 0},

where
\widehat{m}_{1}(t,{\bf v},\cdot,\cdot)
,
\widehat{\bf m}_{2}(t,{\bf v},\cdot,\cdot)
,
\widehat{\bf m}_{12}(t,{\bf v},\cdot,\cdot)
are estimators for the derivatives of
m(t,{\bf v},\cdot,\cdot)
with respect to the first two elements given in Supplement
1.2.1
.

Finally, we estimate
m\{t,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},I(W\leq t),WI(W\leq t)\}
nonparametrically via
\widehat{\Lambda}_{T}\{t-W,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W\}I(W\leq t)+\Lambda_{N}\{t,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}\}\{1-I(W\leq t)\}
based on a kernel
smoothed version of the Nelson-Aalen estimator
(
Ramlau-Hansen 1983
;
Andersen et al. 1993
)
. For any
t
,
W
with
W<t
, and
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}
, the estimators,
\widehat{\Lambda}_{T}\{t-W,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W\}
and
\widehat{\Lambda}_{N}\{t,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}\}
, have the forms of

\displaystyle\widehat{\Lambda}_{T}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{I(W_{i}\leq s)K_{h}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W_{i}-W)}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}Y_{j}(s)I(W_{j}\leq s)K_{h}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W_{j}-W)}dN_{i}(s),

\displaystyle\widehat{\Lambda}_{N}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{I(W_{i}>s)K_{h}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}Y_{j}(s)I(W_{j}>s)K_{h}(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})}dN_{i}(s).

Following
Maguluri and
Zhang 1994
, we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{T}(t-W,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\widehat{\Lambda}_{T}(t-W,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)}\int_{t-W}^{\infty}e^{-\widehat{\Lambda}_{T}(s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)}ds;{\rm when}\,W<t;

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{N}(t,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\widehat{\Lambda}_{N}(t,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})}\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-\widehat{\Lambda}_{N}(s,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})}ds;{\rm when}\,W>t.

We show in Supplement
2
that the estimators of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
are root-
n
consistent, asymptotically normally distributed and semiparametrically
efficient, and the nonparametric estimators,
\widehat{m}_{T}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)

and
\widehat{m}_{N}(\cdot,\cdot)
, are asymptotically normally distributed.

4
Simulation

The section features four simulation studies for evaluating the
finite sample performance of our
method in estimating
\boldsymbol{\beta}
and

m(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})
. For comparisons, we additionally
implement a semiparametric proportional mean
residual life model, denoted as “PM”
(
Chen and Cheng 2005
)
.
This competing method implicitly assumes
d=1
.

Study 1:

We generate event times with hazard functions of

\lambda_{N}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})=te^{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}}
and

\lambda_{T}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)=\frac{10e^{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}+W}+1}{t+1}

so that the true mean residual life is

\displaystyle m_{N}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\frac{t^{2}}{2e^{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}}}}\Phi\left(-\frac{t}{\sqrt{e^{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}}}}\right)\sqrt{2\pi},

\displaystyle m_{T}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{t+1}{10e^{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}+W}},

where
\Phi
is the probability function of standard normal distribution.
Each component in
{\bf X}
is generated independently from the
standard normal
distribution. The waiting time
W
is generated independently from a uniform distribution over
[0,10]
. We consider
d=1,p=9
and
set the true parameters
to be
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(1,-0.6,0.0,-0.3,-0.1,0.0,0.1,0.3,-0.5)^{\rm T}
. The sample size is
n=300
and we randomly assign around one third of samples to take the transplant.

Study 2:

We generate event times with hazard functions of

\lambda_{N}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})=\frac{2t}{e^{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}}+t^{2}}
and

\lambda_{T}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)=\phi\{\ln(t)-3-W/100+0.1(1-\sqrt{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})^{2}\}/t[\Phi\{-\ln(t)+3+W/100-0.1(1-\sqrt{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})^{2}\}]

so that the true mean residual life is

\displaystyle m_{N}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1+\frac{t^{2}}{e^{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}}}\right)\left\{\frac{\pi}{2}-\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{t}{e^{\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}}}\right)\right\},

\displaystyle m_{T}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi\left\{-\ln(t)+3+W/100-0.1(1-\sqrt{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\times\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\Phi\{3+W/100-\ln(s)-0.1(1-\sqrt{2}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})^{2}\}}ds,

where
\phi
is the probability density function of standard normal distribution.
Each component in
{\bf X}
is generated independently from the
standard normal
distribution and
W
is generated independently from uniform distribution over
[0,200]
. We consider
d=1,p=9
and
set the true parameters
to be
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(1,-0.6,0,-0.3,-0.1,0,0.1,0.3,-0.5)^{\rm T}
. The sample size is
n=1000
and we randomly assign around one third of samples to take the transplant.

Study 3:
The hazard functions are generated from
\lambda_{N}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})=t^{2/5}\sum_{i=1}^{d}e^{{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}}
and

\lambda_{T}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)=t^{7/5}W\sum_{i=1}^{d}e^{{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}}.

The corresponding mean residual lives are

\displaystyle m_{N}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\frac{5}{7}t^{7/5}\sum_{i=1}^{d}e^{{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}}}\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{5}{7}s^{7/5}\sum_{i=1}^{d}e^{{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}}}ds,

\displaystyle m_{T}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\frac{5}{12}t^{12/5}\sum_{i=1}^{d}e^{{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}}}W\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{5}{12}s^{12/5}\sum_{i=1}^{d}e^{{\boldsymbol{\beta}_{i}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}}}ds.

Each component in
{\bf X}
is generated independently from the
standard normal
distribution. The waiting time
W
is generated independently from a uniform distribution over
(0,1)
. We consider
d=2,p=6
and
set the true parameters
to be

\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\cdot 1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\cdot 2})=((1,0,-0.65,\allowbreak-0.5,-0.25,0.25)^{\rm T},(0,1,-0.5,0.5,-0.4,0.25)^{\rm T})^{\rm T}
. The sample size is
n=2000
and we randomly assign around one third of samples to take the transplant.

Study 4:
This study mimics the real data application. The hazards are set to be

\displaystyle\lambda_{N}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})=\frac{1}{200}e^{t/200+\arctan(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})+\pi/2}-\frac{1}{200},

and

\displaystyle\lambda_{T}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)=\frac{1}{300}e^{t/300+\arctan(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}-W/5+10)+\pi/2}-\frac{1}{300}.

The corresponding mean residual lives are

\displaystyle m_{N}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 200e^{-t/200-\arctan(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X})-\pi/2},

\displaystyle m_{T}(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X},W)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 300e^{-t/300-\arctan(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf X}-W/5+10)-\pi/2}.

We consider
d=1,p=9
and
set the true parameters
to be

\boldsymbol{\beta}=(0.4,1,-0.4,-1.50,\allowbreak-1.1,1.4,-0.1,-0.7)^{\rm T}
. The transplantation time
W
is generated from the uniform distribution on
(0,\max(T_{N}))
. The sample size is
n=2,000
with a censoring rate of 26%, and about half of the samples receive the transplantation during followup. Study 4 mimics the features of real data that the mean residual life functions are decreasing gradually as
t
increases. The transplant group accounts for
W
: the improvement
m_{T}(t-W,{\bf X},W)-m_{N}(t,{\bf X})
is negative when
W
is close to 0, and approaches 0 positively as
W
increases.

The results for the estimation of

\boldsymbol{\beta}
under Study 1 are given in Table

1
, with three
censoring rates, 0%, 20% and 40%.
The proposed
method has much smaller biases and standard deviations, whereas
“PM" is
biased with larger standard deviations.
The performances of all of the estimators deteriorate when the censoring rate increases, though our method still outperforms the others.
We also
demonstrate the true and error plots
in Figure
1
–
3
, demonstrate
that our method fare well for
estimating
m(t,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf x})
when
t
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf x}
are not too extreme.
The contour plots reveal that bias
increases as censoring rate increases and the estimation
deteriorates when
t
is large.
These results show an overall satisfactory performance of our semiparametric method.
Figure
1
–
3
reveals that the performance of our method is better when
t
is in the
interior of the range because more observations are
available for
the local estimation, as opposed to a larger
t
with fewer observations available.
In contrast,
regardless of the magnitude of
t
, the mean residual
life function estimated by “PM” is severely biased, as shown
in the last two rows from Figure
1
–
3
. This is because
this model assume a pre-determined functional
form of the mean residual life, which in this case is misspecified.

. Our proposed method outperforms the competitor across various scenarios. This difference in performance is particularly evident when

, where the PM method struggles to accurately estimate the mean residual life function of the transplanted objects. This discrepancy can be attributed to the PM method’s limited ability to handle the nonlinear structure inherent in the mean residual life function.

5
Analysis of the Kidney Transplant Data

We apply the proposed method to analyze a kidney transplant data
set from the
U.S. Scientific Registry of Transplant Recipients (SRTR) mentioned in the introduction. Briefly, the registry is
maintained by the United Network for Organ Sharing and Organ Procurement and
Transplantation Network (UNOS/OPTN) and includes all waitlisted
kidney transplant candidates and transplant recipients in the U. S. (
https://unos.org/
). For assessing possible benefits of transplantation, we use the residual life to estimate how much longer a patient can
survive if she or he receives a transplant than otherwise.

To avoid confounding cohort effects and also to have a sufficiently long followup, we focus on the patients who were waitlisted in the same year of 2011. There were 43,140 patients in this cohort with an average followup of 907 days after waitlisting. During the followup, a total of 22,183
patients received kidney transplants. The
response variable is the survival time in days
(
T_{i}
) starting from waitlisting.
Among patients who got a transplantation, 5.86% of the observations were
censored, and the censoring rate was
26.43% among those without a transplantation. The covariates
{\bf X}
included in our analysis were
gender (
X_{1}
), race (
X_{2}
), max cold ischemia time
(
X_{3}
),
insurance coverage (
X_{4}
), body mass index (
X_{5}
),
diagnosis
type (
X_{6}
), peak PRA/CPRA (
X_{7}
), previous malignancy
status
(
X_{8}
) and diabetes indicator (
X_{9}
), all of which were used for computing the EPTS score
(
Time 2012
)
. The waiting time
W
is also considered in our model as proposed in (
1
) and (
).
Our analytical goal was to use model (
) to
quantify the potential residual life increment if a patient
receives a kidney transplant given the covariate profile.
The model mimics a real waitlisting to transplantation process by stipulating that all of the patients started by belonging in the non-transplant group, while those who got a transplantation were viewed as censored at transplantation; once transplanted, a patient would switch his or her membership to join the transplant group.

To proceed, we first determine the number of indices
d

using the Validated Information Criterion (VIC)
(
Ma and Zhang 2015
)
,
where the smallest VIC value corresponds to the selected
d
value.
In our analysis,
d=1
is chosen with the smallest
\text{VIC}=143.66
, indicating a single index is sufficiently informative; see Table
5
.
Subsequently, we normalize the index vector by fixing the first component (gender) at 1, and report 8 coefficient estimates.
All of the covariates, except for the max cold ischemia time (
X_{3}
) and the
body mass index (
X_{5}
), have significant effects
on the mean residual life, which agrees with the
previous studies

(
Friedman
et al. 2003
;
Webster et al. 2017
)
.

Table 5:
Parameter estimation of the kidney transplant data. “est." is the estimation of parameter, “s.d."
is the estimated standard deviation of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
.

p-value

0.000

0.000

0.000

0.000

0.008

The max cold ischemia time
(
X_{3}
) that refers to the tolerable amount of time from when a kidney is removed from the donor to the time it is transplanted into the recipient. Although the max cold ischemia time reflects the patient’s physiological conditions indirectly, it is not as significant as the real cold ischemia time in determining the post-operative risk
(
Iida et al. 2008
;
Kayler et al. 2011
)
. BMI (
X_{5}
) is commonly suggested as a “paradox” risk factor in the literature
(
Kalantar-Zadeh
et al. 2005
;
Ahmadi et al. 2016
)
. A popular
explanation is that the BMI cannot differentiate between fat
and muscle, thus high BMI patients may gain a survival
advantage
(
Beddhu 2004
;
Mafra, Guebre-Egziabher and
Fouque 2008
)
.

On the other hand, race (
X_{2}
) and insurance coverage (
X_{4}
) have significant impacts on survival.
It has been widely accepted that race and insurance
coverage are highly correlated with patients’ socioeconomic status, which plays an
crucial role in the choice of chronic kidney disease treatment, especially for the end-stage patients
(
Lewis et al. 2010
;
Muntner et al. 2012
;
Nicholas, Kalantar-Zadeh and
Norris 2013
;
Webster et al. 2017
)
.
The other significant variables are also known risk factors for the ESRD mortality in the literature
(
Kauffman
et al. 2005
;
Mehdi and Toto 2009
;
Kayler et al. 2011
;
Pyram et al. 2012
;
Lim, Chapman and Wong 2015
)
.

Given a patient with characteristics

{\bf x}
, alive at time
t
, and waiting time
w
,

\widehat{m}_{T}(t-w,\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x},w)-\widehat{m}_{N}(t,\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})

provides an estimate of the patient’s mean residual life improvement after receiving a kidney transplant at
w
. Because the
difference is a function of
t
,

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}
and
w
, we present
the
difference using various
plots. Figure
10
plots contours that
change with
t
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}
at several fixed

w
values.

Fig 10:
Mean residual life improvement from UNOS/OPTN data. Panels from left to right:
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf x}=-0.8,0,0.8
.

Several important observations can be made.
First, with the waiting time being close to 0 in each panel of Figure
10
, kidney transplant led to less survival gains compared to dialysis treatment, possibly
because patients transplanted without waiting were likely to be high-risk patients and
postoperative complications, such as cardiovascular and urological complications, increase mortality risk among them
(
Rahnemai-Azar, Gilchrist and
Kayler 2015
;
den Dekker et al. 2020
)
. Second,
as the waiting time
w
increases, kidney transplant could result in a reasonably larger improvement compared to dialysis. This is because these patients tended to be more stable, allowing kidney transplant to provide a notable survival advantage
(
Ingsathit
et al. 2013
;
Schold et al. 2014
;
Bui, Kilambi and
Mehrotra 2019
)
. Moreover, with
t
and
w
fixed, complex relationships existed between the patient’s index value
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf x}
and the survival improvement. The improvement is larger at
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf x}=0
than that at
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf x}=-0.8,0.8
. It is very likely that large or small values of
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf x}
were resulted by extreme health conditions which led to the worse improvement. Thus, this index in general measured patients’ overall health condition.

African American
Female,Public
Female,Private
Male,Public
Male,Private
Hispanic
Native
Asian
White

Mean residual life improvement from UNOS/OPTN data. Stratified by Race, Gender, and Insurance Status with minimum
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf x}
per strata.

Mean residual life improvement from UNOS/OPTN data. Stratified by Race, Gender, and Insurance Status with median
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\rm T}{\bf x}
per strata.

African American
